````text
AFTER THE LAST WORD — One hard blink. The eyes stay on him and go glassier. A long uneven exhale. Her shoulder line lowers two or three centimetres and her chin comes back down a degree. She holds his look to the last frame.
SPEECH ARTICULATION: driven by the audio file only. The mouth and jaw do the work of the words, but the whole lower face participates — cheek, chin, the muscles at the corners of the mouth, the small movements of the throat — as it does in real speech. No head-nodding on stressed words, no eyebrow punctuation, no theatrical emphasis. The performance is under the words, not on them.
HER GAZE stays on him for the whole shot. If it leaves at any point it is only for a fraction of a second, dropping a few degrees and coming straight back — never a look away, never a look at the lens.

PERFORMANCE — HIS REACTIONS. HE IS ALIVE, HE TAKES IT, AND HE IS COMPLETELY SILENT.
He is out of focus and faceless, so every reaction must be legible as a change in a soft dark silhouette: the height and angle of his shoulder, the line of his neck, the tilt of the back of his head, the loose edge of his hair, the visible rate of his breathing. Play them large enough in the body to read at this softness, but never large enough to become melodrama. He is the second performance in this shot, not a piece of set dressing.
CRITICAL: HIS PERFORMANCE IS PURELY VISUAL. He breathes visibly but INAUDIBLY. Everything he does must read with the eye alone, because nothing he does makes a sound. Do not compensate for the silence by making his movements bigger.
HIS BASELINE, CONTINUOUS: breathing steadily and visibly, shoulder rising and falling, small continuous postural life — the micro-sway of a torso on a chair, the constant minute corrections of a real neck. He is never a static shape in the foreground, not for a single frame.
BEFORE SHE SPEAKS — One slow breath. His shoulder settles a centimetre. He is waiting for it.
ON "You said there was room." — He goes still. The breathing stops mid-cycle and the shoulder holds at the top of an inhale a beat too long. The stillness itself is the reaction, and it is only legible because he was moving before it.
IN THE FIRST PAUSE — The held breath releases, slowly and unevenly, visible in the shoulder dropping. The back of his head lowers a centimetre.
ON "Somebody else." — The head turns a few degrees away from her, toward screen-left, then comes back — a man who wanted to look away and did not. The loose edge of his hair shifts and settles with the movement.
IN THE SECOND PAUSE — His shoulder rises: not breath this time but tension, the trapezius pulling up as he braces for the next one. One slow deliberate breath in and out, read entirely from the shoulder line. One small weight shift on the chair, the whole silhouette resettling a centimetre lower than it was.
ON "You decide who lives." — THE FLINCH. The shoulder contracts a centimetre and releases, instantly suppressed. It is small, it is involuntary, and it is the only large thing his body does in the whole shot. His head drops a fraction with it.
AFTER IT, to 0:10 — He holds, the shoulder rising and falling shallow and uneven. One more small settle. He does not answer, does not stand, does not lean in, does not reach out, does not turn away. The silhouette stays exactly where it is, alive and taking it.
At no point does he come into focus, show his face, or turn toward the lens.
````

## scene-01-21 · A man sits at a table in a small apartment, facing his wife across it.

`PERFORMANCE — THE ONE ACTION OF THE SHOT` · Close-up · Locked-off · 6s

Used in: [scene-01-21](../shots/scene-01/21_a-man-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-facing.md)

````text
THE ENTIRE SHOT IS ONE EVENT: HE LOWERS HIS EYES. Everything else exists to make that event land.
He begins holding her look, and the holding is already costing him. The controlling emotion is SUPPRESSED GRIEF WEARING PROCEDURE AS ARMOUR, WITH SHAME LEAKING THROUGH THE JOINTS. He is not cold, not angry, not blank. He is a man who has run out of the ability to keep his eyes on the person he has helped destroy.
THE LOOK DOWN IS A DEFEAT, NOT A GESTURE. He does not look away sideways, does not turn his head away, does not break the frontality. The eyes go down and stay down. It is the moment the armour fails.
THE DESCENT HAPPENS IN TWO STAGES WITH A HESITATION BETWEEN THEM: the lids and the gaze start down a few degrees and stop, as though he might come back up; a held beat where he almost does; then the rest of the way, slower than the first stage, all the way down. The second stage is the one that reads as giving up.
THE EYES LEAD AND THE HEAD FOLLOWS. The gaze goes first. A beat later the head lowers by roughly a centimetre, the neck absorbing the difference, arriving after the eyes rather than with them. This is never a nod and never a bow — the chin drops a fraction, not toward the chest.
THE GAZE LANDS ON NOTHING. It comes to rest low and slightly toward screen-right, on the unseen table or on nothing at all, unfocused, not looking at an object. It does not return to her eyes at any point before 0:06.
AFTER IT LANDS he is not released. The lids stay heavy, the brow does not lift, and the shame stays visible in the stillness of the face. The eyes remain wet with tear film catching light, glassy at the lower lid, but NO TEAR FALLS and the eyes never redden into crying.
He never plays "sad". No performed sorrow, no mouth turning down, no crumpling face, no theatrical grief. The sadness is legible only in the lids, the throat, the breath and the loss of the gaze.

PERFORMANCE — HIS BODY. CONTINUOUS LIVING MOVEMENT, NEVER A LOCKED TORSO.
His body is in constant low-amplitude motion for all 6 seconds. This is not fidgeting and not swaying: it is the normal, irregular, unrepeating movement of a real person sitting upright under strain. Nothing about him is ever held in place.
FORWARD AND BACKWARD DRIFT — the spine is never a fixed column. His torso travels slowly toward and away from her, 2–4cm at a time, driven from the hips and lower back rather than from the neck. The drift is slow, irregular and unrepeating: never a rhythm, never a rock, never returning to the same position twice. Each move has real acceleration and deceleration, with a small overshoot and settle, never a linear glide.
NECK AND HEAD — the head is never locked to the torso. It drifts by millimetres with the breathing and makes the constant involuntary corrections of a real neck holding a head upright. It lags a fraction behind the torso on any lean: the chest moves first, the head follows a beat later.
SHOULDERS — alive and asymmetric, rising and falling visibly and irregularly with the uneven breathing. One rolls back and settles once across the take, a real muscular release. They drop a centimetre as the gaze completes its descent and are not fully pulled back up. At no point are the two shoulders level and still at the same time.
TORSO AND BREATH — the chest expands and contracts unevenly under the linen, the ribcage visibly working, abdomen and lower chest participating. One breath catches at the top and is released deliberately.
ARMS — his hands stay below the bottom edge of frame at all times, but his arms are not inert. Movement below frame reads in the visible upper body: the deltoid and upper arm shift as an unseen forearm moves; one shoulder drops slightly as an arm resettles; the linen at the upper arm creases and releases. Twice across the take an unseen arm movement travels up through the shoulder as a small displacement — once early, once as the gaze lands.
WEIGHT — one real weight shift as he settles his hips into the chair early in the take, the whole torso reorganising around it, shoulders and head adjusting a fraction afterward.
The movement is most contained while he is still holding her eyes, and loosens fractionally after the gaze goes down — the body letting go a little once the effort of holding the look has been abandoned.
AMPLITUDE LOCK: every movement described here is small. Centimetres of torso travel, millimetres of head movement. Nothing reads as a gesture, a shift of position, a rock, or a lean. A viewer should register him as still and alive, never as moving.
````

## scene-01-21 · A man sits at a table in a small apartment, facing his wife across it.

`PERFORMANCE — HIS FACE, MECHANICS` · Close-up · Locked-off · 6s

Used in: [scene-01-21](../shots/scene-01/21_a-man-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-facing.md)

````text
He is not blank and he is not stone. The effort shows as continuous low-level micro-activity, not as expressions. No expression change ever "lands" cleanly.
While the eyes are still up: micro-saccades flicker as they hold on her, tiny involuntary shifts between her left eye and her right. Pupils dilate slightly in the low light. Lower lids sit fractionally tense. Blinking is irregular, never metronomic — one fast blink, a long gap, one slow heavy one.
The lower lids tighten a fraction just before the descent begins — the first visible sign that he is about to lose the look.
As the gaze goes down, the upper lids come with it and stay low. One blink lands inside the descent, slow and heavy.
Two dry swallows travel the throat across the take, the larynx moving visibly, one of them immediately before the eyes go down. Nostrils flare a fraction on each intake.
Involuntary tells scattered across the six seconds, never repeating in the same place: the masseter tightening once under the beard; the brow drawing in a millimetre and releasing; a tiny twitch at one corner of the mouth suppressed instantly; a single micro-flinch through the cheek as she moves.
No head-nodding, no eyebrow punctuation, no theatrical emphasis. The performance sits under the silence.
````

## scene-01-21 · A man sits at a table in a small apartment, facing his wife across it.

`PERFORMANCE — HER. SHE ANSWERS WITH HER BODY.` · Close-up · Locked-off · 6s

Used in: [scene-01-21](../shots/scene-01/21_a-man-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-facing.md)

````text
She is out of focus and faceless, so every reaction must read as a change in a soft dark silhouette: the height and angle of the shoulder, the line of the neck, the tilt of the back of the head, the loose strands at the nape, the rate of her breathing. Play each beat large enough in the body to read at this softness, never large enough to become melodrama.
BASELINE — breathing steadily, shoulder rising and falling, small continuous postural life. She is not a statue in the foreground.
WHILE HE HOLDS HER EYES — she is waiting. The breathing goes shallow and quick. The shoulder lifts fractionally: not breath, tension, the trapezius pulling up as she braces. The neck lengthens as her chin lifts a few degrees toward him.
AS HIS GAZE STARTS DOWN — she goes still. The breathing stops mid-cycle, the shoulder frozen at the top of an inhale and held a beat too long. The stillness is the reaction.
AS THE GAZE COMPLETES — the whole silhouette leans in toward the table by a few centimetres, the shoulder coming closer to the lens and growing softer as it does. She is going after his eyes and he is not there.
LAST BEAT — the held breath releases slowly and unevenly, one shoulder drops as an arm moves below frame, and the lean settles back a fraction. The back of her head stays turned toward him. She does not look away.
She never comes into focus, never shows her face, never turns toward the lens.
````

## scene-01-22 · A woman sits at a table in a small apartment, holding back tears, her eyes on the window.

`PROFILE — THE DEFINING GEOMETRY OF THE SHOT` · Close-up · Locked-off · 8s

Used in: [scene-01-22](../shots/scene-01/22_a-woman-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-holdi.md)

````text
She is in strict left-facing profile for the entire 8 seconds. The camera sees the side of her head, not the front.
Her forehead, the bridge and tip of her nose, the philtrum, the lips, the chin and the underside of the jaw read as ONE CONTINUOUS SILHOUETTE EDGE against the background at screen-left. That edge is the compositional spine of the frame.
Only ONE eye is visible — her near eye, the camera-side eye — seen from the side. Her far eye is completely hidden behind the bridge of her nose. The near eye reads through the profile of the lids, the lashes in side view, and the wet curve of the eyeball itself.
The line of her neck and throat runs from under the jaw down to the collarbone, fully readable.
The low knot of her hair sits behind, at the back of the head, with the loose strands at temple and nape hanging free.
She does not turn toward the camera at any point. She does not turn away. The profile does not break, not on the breath catch, not on the gesture, not at the end. If her head moves, it moves within a degree or two and returns.
````

## scene-01-22 · A woman sits at a table in a small apartment, holding back tears, her eyes on the window.

`PERFORMANCE — THE EMOTIONAL STATE` · Close-up · Locked-off · 8s

Used in: [scene-01-22](../shots/scene-01/22_a-woman-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-holdi.md)

````text
She is holding back tears and she is losing. That is the entire content of the shot.
This is not crying. It is the fight not to cry, which is a different physical event: the body trying to suppress a response that is already underway. The tears are present before the shot begins. What we watch is the suppression failing by degrees, and one small involuntary act of self-management.
She is not performing grief for anyone. She is alone with it, looking at a window because looking at a window is easier than looking at a person. There is nothing to see out there. The window is somewhere to put her eyes.
Because we only have her profile, the performance must live in what a profile can carry: the jaw, the throat, the chin, the corner of the mouth, the single visible eye, the breath in the chest and shoulder. Play every beat in those places. Nothing that depends on seeing both eyes or the full face will read.
Nothing lands cleanly. No expression arrives on a beat. Everything is continuous, involuntary and slightly behind itself.
````

## scene-01-22 · A woman sits at a table in a small apartment, holding back tears, her eyes on the window.

`PERFORMANCE — FACE MECHANICS IN PROFILE` · Close-up · Locked-off · 8s

Used in: [scene-01-22](../shots/scene-01/22_a-woman-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-holdi.md)

````text
Continuous low-level involuntary activity throughout, never a still face and never an expression that arrives whole. Everything below must be legible from the side.
THE NEAR EYE: wet and glassy for all 8 seconds. In profile the wet reads as a bright curved highlight along the exposed surface of the eyeball and a shining line at the lower lash line. The lashes are separated and readable in side view. Micro-saccades as she looks at nothing in particular — the eye settles, drifts a fraction, settles again. Irregular asymmetric blinking, never metronomic: one slow heavy blink early, a fast one after the caught breath, one slow one at the end. The skin of the lower lid goes faintly pink across the take — the real colour change of held crying, not makeup.
THE SILHOUETTE EDGE carries the expression. Watch it change shape: the brow lowering a millimetre and lifting at its inner end; the nostril flaring very slightly on the caught breath; the lips pressing and releasing; the chin dimpling faintly two or three times as the mentalis engages involuntarily, each time smoothing out; the corner of the mouth pulling down a fraction once and being pulled back. These are the only expressive events and they are all tiny.
THE JAW AND THROAT do more work here than in any other angle. The masseter engages and releases at the hinge, visible as a change in definition. Two swallows travel up the throat, the larynx and the muscles under the jaw moving clearly in side view. The breath catch reads in the throat before it reads anywhere else.
No single expression ever completes. Everything is interrupted, suppressed or half-released.
````

## scene-01-22 · A woman sits at a table in a small apartment, holding back tears, her eyes on the window.

`PERFORMANCE — BODY MECHANICS` · Close-up · Locked-off · 8s

Used in: [scene-01-22](../shots/scene-01/22_a-woman-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-holdi.md)

````text
She is never frozen. Continuous micro-postural life: the small corrections of a seated body, the head drifting by millimetres on the neck, breathing that never becomes regular.
Head and neck: the head is not locked. It makes involuntary corrections and settles a few millimetres lower across the take, the small collapse of someone who has been holding a position. Every one of these movements is small enough that the strict profile is preserved — the far eye never becomes visible, the far cheek never appears.
Shoulders: low, slightly forward, as in the position reference. The near shoulder rises and falls shallowly and unevenly with the breathing at the lower edge of frame. It drops a centimetre as the arm lifts and returns as it comes back down.
The hand movement is the only large motion in the shot: from the table, up into frame from the bottom edge, to the side of her face, held, then down and out. It comes from the shoulder and elbow with real acceleration and deceleration and a small settle. The forearm and the ring are briefly visible as the hand passes. It stays in the lower third of the frame and never occludes the profile edge.
Torso: she does not lean in or back. Her forearms stay on the table apart from the one that lifts. Her seated position at the end is the same as at the start.
Skin: real pores, real freckles, fine lines at the outer corner of the visible eye, the fine down on the cheek and jaw catching the light along the silhouette edge. Never smoothed, never waxy. Where she touches, the skin drags very slightly and springs back.
Hair: the loose strands at her temple and nape hang and move only with her head, as weighted mass, settling after each movement. The knot at the back of the head stays put.
No trembling lip held for effect, no shuddering shoulders, no head shake, no nod, no covering the face, no theatrical grimace, no rubbery deformation, no motion-capture stiffness.
````

## scene-01-23 · A woman sits at a table in a small apartment, holding back tears.

`THE MOVE — THE SPINE OF THE SHOT` · Close-up · Locked-off · 8s

Used in: [scene-01-23](../shots/scene-01/23_a-woman-sits-at-a-table-in-a-small-apartment-holdi.md)

````text
This shot has exactly one structural event: she turns her head from him to the window.
START POSITION, three-quarter: her head is turned toward screen-right, toward her husband who is off frame at the far side of the table. The camera sees her in three-quarter front — both eyes visible, the near cheek full, the far cheekbone and the far corner of the eye readable. She is looking at him and she is holding it.
END POSITION, strict profile: her head is turned to screen-left, toward the window. Forehead, the bridge and tip of the nose, the philtrum, the lips, the chin and the underside of the jaw read as ONE CONTINUOUS SILHOUETTE EDGE against the dark background. Only her near eye — the camera-side eye — is visible; the far eye is completely hidden behind the bridge of the nose.
The rotation between the two is roughly 45–55°, and it happens ONCE. She does not turn back. She does not check on him again. Once she is on the window she stays on the window for the rest of the take.
This is not a look-away of avoidance played for the camera. It is a woman who cannot hold someone's eyes any longer and puts her eyes somewhere neutral so that her face can come apart without being watched.
````

What we watch is the suppression failing by degrees, the turn it forces, and one small involuntary act of self-management afterwards.
````text
In the first seconds she is holding two things at once: the tears, and his eyes. She can only manage one. The turn is what it costs her to keep managing the tears.
Once she is on the window she is alone with it. There is nothing to see out there. The window is somewhere to put her eyes.
````

````text
She is never frozen. Continuous micro-postural life: the small corrections of a seated body, the head drifting by millimetres on the neck, breathing that never becomes regular.
THE TURN is anatomically correct: the neck initiates, the head follows, the chin travels through a slight downward arc rather than sweeping level, and there is a small settle at the end rather than a hard stop. ## scene-02-02 · A man puts on his uniform alone in a dark red locker room.
````

`SHOT LIST AND ACTION TIMING` · Medium · Handheld · 20s

Used in: [scene-02-02](../shots/scene-02/02_a-man-puts-on-his-uniform-alone-in-a-dark-red-lock.md)

````text
SHOT 1 — MEDIUM FROM BEHIND, 5s
Camera close behind him, 50mm, framing him from the waist to just above the head. The open locker door beside him at frame right, its interior a black rectangle with an empty chrome hook. Red locker panels fill the rest of the background, soft.
First frame: his back in the black base layer, the olive jacket already in both hands in front of him, out of sight below the frame edge.
0:00–0:02 he works his right arm into the jacket sleeve — the shoulder rotating back, the elbow driving through, the heavy fabric resisting. The camera drifts right with the shoulder rotation, arriving a beat late.
0:02–0:03.2 he swings the jacket around behind him and finds the second sleeve, the same movement mirrored. The camera swings with it, a small lateral correction that overshoots slightly and comes back. The jacket falls onto his shoulders with real weight.
0:03.2–0:04.3 he settles it: one shrug, both shoulders lifting and dropping, the fabric arranging itself across his back. The frame rises with the shrug and settles after it. He tugs the hem down once with both hands; the camera tilts down a few degrees to follow the hands, then comes back up.
0:04.3–0:05 stillness in him, not in the frame. His head is down.

SHOT 2 — CHEST DETAIL, 4s
Tight on his chest, three-quarter angle, no face in frame — the shot ends at the dark high collar. The operator is very close on the 50mm; the handheld float is at its most visible here, the frame moving several centimetres and correcting continuously. Shallow depth of field, background dissolved to a soft dark red field.
First frame: the vest already over the jacket, hanging open at the front. Both bare hands at the front closures, mid-action.
0:00–0:02 the fastenings engage one after another, fingers pressing, the front panels drawing together and flattening across the chest. The camera tracks the working hands down the chest as they move, a small vertical follow.
0:02–0:03 he presses the closed panel down with the heel of one hand. The frame steadies briefly on the press.
0:03–0:04 the clipped ID card swings once against the vest plate; the camera drifts a few degrees toward it and damps as the card damps. The hands leave frame downward and the camera goes with them, then stops.

SHOT 3 — PROFILE CLOSE-UP, 4s
Right-facing profile on the 50mm, close — his head fills the left half of the frame, framed from the shoulders up. He faces the open locker at screen-right; the black rectangle of the locker interior fills the right of frame behind him, the darkest area in the sequence. Handheld, so the profile is not a rigid graphic silhouette: the angle wanders a few degrees and the operator keeps finding it again. At 50mm this close there is mild perspective on the features, not telephoto flattening.
First frame: his profile against the black cavity — brow, nose, lips, beard line as a clean edge. The cold strip is out of frame above; its light catches the top of his head, the brow ridge and the bridge of the nose.
0:00–0:01.3 he looks into the locker, level. One blink, lashes readable. The vest settles onto his shoulders — the shoulder line drops a centimetre as the weight arrives, and the camera dips fractionally with it.
0:01.3–0:02.6 a small saccade: the eyes shift 2–3° and return, the return slower than the departure. The jaw tightens fractionally at the hinge — 1–2mm of masseter definition under the beard — and holds. The operator leans in a few centimetres, closing the frame slightly.
0:02.6–0:04 a single dry swallow, the larynx rising and dropping once in profile. One more blink. The expression does not change at any point.

SHOT 4 — HANDS INSERT, 3s
Very close on his hands at waist height, 50mm, camera angled slightly down. Dark red locker panels dissolved behind. Shallow depth of field — glove fabric and knuckle texture sharp.
First frame: both hands in frame, one glove already half on.
0:00–0:01.8 he works the glove down finger by finger, the fabric stretching over the knuckles and resisting at the base of the thumb. The frame follows the hands as they shift position, holding them roughly centred but never precisely.
0:01.8–0:02.4 the other hand pulls the cuff into place over the jacket sleeve.
0:02.4–0:03 he flexes the hand once, closed and open, and it stills. The frame keeps moving.

SHOT 5 — CLOSE ON THE HAND AND DOOR, 2s
Close on his gloved right hand and the edge of the open locker door, 50mm, the black cavity behind soft. No face in frame.
0:00–0:01 the hand pushes. The door swings shut away from it, accelerating through the arc, meeting the frame and stopping dead. No bounce, no rattle. The camera follows the door across — a small pan right that arrives a beat late and overshoots slightly as the door stops.
0:01–0:02 the glove stays flat on the closed metal. The frame corrects back onto the hand and holds, drifting.

SHOT 6 — LATERAL MEDIUM, PROFILE, 2s
He stands in right-facing profile beside the now-closed locker, 50mm, framed from the chest to above the crown, filling the left half of frame. The unbroken red wall behind him, soft. The bench crosses the lower foreground, empty.
First frame: fully dressed — jacket, vest closed, gloves on, no helmet. His right arm still extended toward the closed door at screen-right, his eyes level, aimed where the cavity used to be.
0:00–0:01.2 he does not look at his hand. One blink. The frame drifts and is corrected once.
0:01.2–0:02 the arm lowers; the camera tilts down a few degrees with the falling hand, then comes back up to him. His head lowers 1–2cm. He stays where he is, still in profile, still facing the closed locker. The sequence ends there.
````

## scene-02-03 · A man stands at a washroom sink, hands on the porcelain, staring at nothing.

`SHOT LIST AND ACTION TIMING` · Medium · Handheld · 20s

Used in: [scene-02-03](../shots/scene-02/03_a-man-stands-at-a-washroom-sink-hands-on-the-porce.md)

````text
SHOT 1 — WIDE, FROM THE DOORWAY, 5s
Shot from inside the locker room, through the doorway cut in the locker wall, looking into the washroom. The doorway frames the image within the image: dark red locker panels fill the left and right edges of frame, the washroom visible as a brighter rectangle in the middle. He stands at the basins in three-quarter view, both gloved hands on the porcelain, head down. The mirror strip runs behind him; the fluorescent tube above it is the only light. Roughly 45° diagonal FOV, deep focus.
0:00–0:03 he stands, hands on the basin, unmoving. The handheld frame breathes around him — the doorway edges drifting a degree and correcting. Nothing in the room moves except the camera.
0:03–0:05 his shoulder line rises and falls once with a slow breath. He does not lift his head. The frame settles fractionally tighter.

SHOT 2 — MEDIUM, PROFILE, 6s
Inside the washroom now, at his side. He fills the left half of the frame in near-profile, from the waist up, facing screen-right toward the mirror. The tiled wall, the mirror strip and the tube run away toward the right of frame. His reflection is present in the mirror but soft, dark and partial — a shape, not a readable second face. Roughly 35° diagonal FOV.
0:00–0:02 he is bent slightly forward over the basin, arms locked, weight through the hands. His head is lowered. The frame drifts and corrects.
0:02–0:04 he raises his head — slowly, without effort — until it is level. His eyes are open, aimed forward, but they are not looking at the mirror and not looking at anything. The gaze goes through the glass and stops nowhere. No focus, no target.
0:04–0:06 he holds there. One blink, slow. The jaw carries a faint standing tension at the hinge, visible under the beard. The camera keeps breathing around him.

SHOT 3 — HANDS INSERT, 3s
Low and close on the basin, hands and porcelain filling the frame. The gloved fingers are curled over the front edge, the chrome tap and the wet-dark porcelain behind them, exposed pipework soft below. No face in frame. Roughly 30° diagonal FOV, shallow depth of field.
0:00–0:01.5 the hands, still, taking his weight. The glove leather is stretched taut across the knuckles.
0:01.5–0:02.5 the grip tightens — a small increase in pressure, the knuckles defining, the fingers pressing down into the porcelain edge. Nothing else changes.
0:02.5–0:03 the grip releases back to where it was. It does not let go.

SHOT 4 — CLOSE-UP, FRONTAL PAST THE MIRROR, 6s
Close on his face, frontal, from just to the side of the mirror line so that the camera is in his eyeline without him seeing it — he is looking through the lens, not at it. His face fills the centre of the frame from mid-chest to above the crown. The tube above throws the light down onto him; the tiled red wall behind is soft and dark. Roughly 25° diagonal FOV.
0:00–0:02.5 the empty stare, held. The eyes are open and unfocused, the muscles of the face doing nothing at all. This is the longest stretch of stillness in the sequence and it should feel too long. The handheld frame drifting is the only movement in the image.
0:02.5–0:03.5 a single dry swallow — the throat moving once.
0:03.5–0:05 he inhales, slow and deep — the chest lifting under the vest, the shoulders rising — and lets it go. As the breath releases, the eyes come back: the pupils settle, the focus arrives, and he is present again. Nothing else in the face changes.
0:05–0:06 he lowers his head 2–3cm and looks down at the basin. The shot ends there, on the top of his head and the lowered brow.
````

## scene-02-05 · A man sits alone on the bench of an empty red locker room, finishing dressing, seen entire

`MOTION CONTINUITY — HIGHEST PRIORITY` · Close-up · Dolly / push-in · 20s

Used in: [scene-02-05](../shots/scene-02/05_a-man-sits-alone-on-the-bench-of-an-empty-red-lock.md)

````text
Every movement in this shot is fluid and continuous. The body is never at rest between actions and never holds a pose. From the first frame to the last there is always something in motion.
All motion is unbroken: each movement accelerates smoothly from rest, travels through its arc at an even rate, and decelerates into its end position. Nothing starts instantly. Nothing stops dead. Nothing changes direction abruptly.
Actions overlap and blend into one another. Each task begins while the previous one is still finishing — the hands are already travelling toward the next thing before the last is fully released. There are no discrete beats, no reset positions between tasks, no moment where the body returns to neutral and waits.
All limb movement follows curved arcs through space, not straight lines with corners. Elbows, wrists and shoulders lead and trail one another rather than moving in lockstep.
Every frame differs from the frame before it. No repeated frame, no held frame, no momentary freeze, no micro-stall, no stutter, no pulsing, no ratcheting. No action, pause or hitch recurring at a regular interval — nothing lands on a one-second cycle.
Motion carries natural motion blur consistent with a 180-degree shutter at 24fps: moving limbs, straps and fabric blur across the frame in proportion to their speed. Motion is never crisp and frame-stepped.
Pacing is even across the full 20 seconds. No action is rushed to fit its slot and none is padded to fill one.
He is practised and competent — a man who has done this many times, working without hurry and without hesitation. Every action succeeds on the first attempt.
````

Eye open and unfocused, iris aimed screen-right at nothing. He is the stillness of someone too inside their own head to move.

Tremor is nervous and involuntary — small rapid oscillation, approximately 8–12 cycles per second, low amplitude. Not a shake, not a shiver — a fine uncontrollable quiver that cannot be stopped. Fingers carry the tremor to their tips — fingertip movement slightly larger amplitude than wrist. The hands do not move from their position — they hold their height and orientation while trembling within it. No gripping. No clenching. No reaching. Just suspension and tremor.

Not mechanical — the movement has the quality of someone turning their hands over to look at them, or to offer them. Fingers remain loosely extended throughout the rotation. As hands reach mid-rotation, palm hollow comes into view — water pooled inside becomes visible.

````text
0:04–0:06 — Palms fully facing upward. The tremor begins immediately as rotation completes — involuntary, continuous, barely perceptible nervous micro-vibration. 2–4mm amplitude, irregular rhythm. Not a shake, not a shiver — the tremor of held tension that has nowhere to go. Pooled water in palm hollow shimmers with each tremor cycle. Fingers do not curl or clench. Hands remain at the same height. Tremor holds through final frame.
````

## scene-05-02 · Inside the vertical shaft of a narrow urban alley.

`COMPOSITION — CRITICAL` · Wide · Handheld · 4s

Used in: [scene-05-02](../shots/scene-05/02_inside-the-vertical-shaft-of-a-narrow-urban-alley.md)

````text
The frame is a vertical shaft, not an open sky shot. Read this as a strict layout:
LEFT THIRD: entirely filled by a building facade — wall texture, cage balconies, pipes, conduits, window recesses. No sky on this side. The wall runs from the bottom of frame to the top of frame without interruption.
RIGHT THIRD: entirely filled by the opposing building facade — same treatment. No sky on this side. Wall runs bottom to top.
CENTRE: the vertical gap between the two buildings — and behind it, in depth, the receding face of a further tall building closing the shaft. The single black cord hangs straight down the centre of this gap, running from the top of frame toward the camera below.
TOP CENTRE: a TINY sliver of overcast grey sky — a small irregular patch between the converging rooflines, no more than a few percent of the frame area. Inside that sliver, small and partially obscured: the TAIL of <<<prop_osprey>>>. Nothing else of the aircraft is visible.
The sky must be minimal. The buildings dominate. The image reads as looking up a deep narrow well, not as an aerial shot.
````

## scene-06a-01 · Four tactical operatives advance down a long open-air access corridor in an old high-rise 

`LOCATION` · Close-up · Crane / high angle · 10s

Used in: [scene-06a-01](../shots/scene-06a/01_four-tactical-operatives-advance-down-a-long-open.md)

````text
<<<terrazzoest>>>: a very long open-air access corridor in an old high-rise residential district. The walkway runs so far back that its far end falls into haze and shadow. 100% matches the reference.
RIGHT SIDE — the open edge: a continuous run of large regular rectangular openings in the outer wall, evenly spaced, floor-to-shoulder-height, separated by squat concrete piers, repeating in strict rhythm into the distance. A low concrete parapet across the bottom of each opening, topped with a plain welded steel handrail, paint flaking, rust bleeding at the welds. Through the openings: the dense skyline of the surrounding towers, weathered grey-green and pale ochre concrete crowded shoulder to shoulder, projecting window security cages, wall-mounted air-conditioning units, bamboo drying poles with laundry, hazy sky behind them, no gap between buildings. The towers are slightly overexposed and softened by atmospheric haze against the darker corridor interior.
LEFT SIDE — the dwellings: a long unbroken wall of apartment entrances, evenly spaced — plain steel security gates in front of recessed timber doors, some gates painted green, some rust-red, some bare metal. Between the doors, small barred kitchen windows with frosted or louvred glass, each with a small enamel unit-number plate, all signage illegible. Electrical meter boxes and conduit runs mounted at head height, bundled cable tracing the full length. Domestic overflow against the wall at intervals: a folded drying rack, plastic stools, a mop in a bucket, potted plants, a coiled hose. Water stains and mildew streaking down from above, patched render in mismatched tones.
FLOOR AND SOFFIT: worn concrete floor, faintly damp, a shallow drainage channel running along the base of the right-hand wall. Flat concrete soffit overhead, exposed pipework and a line of dead bare bulb fixtures in wire cages running the length.
FAR END: the corridor terminates at the foot of a staircase — the first flight rising to the left, concrete steps with a plain steel handrail, upper steps out of view. The stairwell is dimmer than the corridor, lit only by weak indirect daylight from above, reading as a dark pocket at the end of the run.
No civilians, no residents, no bystanders anywhere — the corridor is empty of people apart from the four operatives, but dense with the traces of habitation.
````

## scene-06a-01 · Four tactical operatives advance down a long open-air access corridor in an old high-rise 

`AVOID — THE CAMERA STOPPING` · Close-up · Crane / high angle · 10s

Used in: [scene-06a-01](../shots/scene-06a/01_four-tactical-operatives-advance-down-a-long-open.md)

````text
No moment where the camera comes to rest. Not one frame of stillness anywhere in the 10 seconds.
No lock-off, no static frame, no held composition, no pause, no freeze.
Above all, no stop at the fist-halt around 0:05 — when the four operatives stop dead, the camera must keep moving. Do not let the frame settle with them, do not resolve the shot into stillness while they hold, do not treat their halt as a cue for the camera to rest. The stillness is theirs alone; the frame stays alive underneath it.
No easing into stability at the head or tail of the shot — frame one is already in motion and the final frame is still moving.
No moment where the sway decays to zero, no damping, no settling out.
No stretch of frames where the framing repeats — no two frames of this shot are composed identically.
No stabilised passages: the camera does not become smooth for a second and then rough again. The handheld texture is unbroken from 0:00 to 0:10.
````

## scene-06a-01 · Four tactical operatives advance down a long open-air access corridor in an old high-rise 

`MAINTAIN — ALL HANDHELD MOVEMENT` · Close-up · Crane / high angle · 10s

Used in: [scene-06a-01](../shots/scene-06a/01_four-tactical-operatives-advance-down-a-long-open.md)

````text
Every handheld characteristic described above runs continuously and simultaneously for the full duration: the backward gait, the breathing cycle, the sway in all axes, the roll wander and its hand corrections, the breathing working distance, the loose re-found framing. None of these is suspended at any point, including during the halt, during the signals, and during the focus corrections. Handheld is the constant state of this shot, not an effect applied to parts of it.

NEGATIVE: no gimbal, no stabilizer, no steadicam, no tripod, no dolly, no slider, no post-stabilization, no smooth glide, no mechanical float. The camera must never feel rigidly attached to the characters and must never feel machine-stabilised.
````

## scene-08-07 · Frontal close-up of a kneeling older man, hands laced behind his head, face angled down.

`THE LOOK` · Close-up · Handheld · 6s

Used in: [scene-08-07](../shots/scene-08/07_frontal-close-up-of-a-kneeling-older-man-hands-lac.md)

````text
The single event of this shot is his head rising. It happens once, slowly, and it does not reverse.
0:00 to 0:02 — Head bowed, eyes down at the floor. He breathes. Nothing else.
0:02 to 0:04.5 — The head lifts. Slowly and steadily, over two and a half seconds, no hesitation and no jerk. The chin comes up, the neck lengthens, the face rotates up into the light. The eyes stay down for the first part of the movement and then come up last, opening under the brow.
0:04.5 to 0:06 — Held. His eyes are up and they stay up. He looks straight ahead, level, at the height of a standing man's chest and face, toward whoever is in front of him offscreen. He does not blink for the first second of the hold, then blinks once, slowly.
The gaze is level, not raised toward the ceiling. He is looking at people, not up at them. It is not pleading, not defiant, not searching. It is a man deciding to see.
He does not look away, does not lower his head again, does not look at the camera lens. His eyeline passes slightly to one side of the lens, toward a point just past it.
His hands stay laced behind his head throughout. The lift comes from the neck and the spine, not from the arms. His knees do not move from their spot on the floorboards.
No sudden snap up, no flinch, no head shake, no glance around the room, no scanning, no second movement.
````

## scene-08-08 · Detail shot of an older man's hands. One hand comes down from behind his head and grips th

`THE GESTURE` · Close-up · Handheld · 8s

````text
One continuous action in four beats. The two hands do not move at the same time: they take turns, because one has to hold what the other works on.
0:00 to 0:01.5 — Held. The right hand rests laced behind the nape, fingers interlocked, knuckles pale from holding. Small tremor in the fingers. He breathes.
0:01.5 to 0:03 — The right hand releases the lace and comes down. Slowly, deliberately, not dropping. The fingers uncurl on the way. It comes forward and closes around the left forearm, just below the elbow, gripping firmly from above. The grip is a support: he is holding his own arm steady.
0:03 to 0:04.5 — The left arm comes down from behind the head and is brought forward, guided by the right hand's grip. It rotates as it comes, so the inner wrist turns upward. The forearm settles roughly horizontal in front of his chest, palm up.
0:04.5 to 0:06.5 — The right hand slides down the left forearm to the cuff, pinches the fabric, and draws the sleeve back toward the elbow. The fabric bunches and resists, then gives. Two short pulls, not one smooth motion. The inner wrist is uncovered.
0:06.5 to 0:08 — Held. <<<birthcode>>> is fully visible on the inner wrist, centered in frame, in focus. The right hand stays on the pushed-back sleeve. The forearm is offered up and stays there, trembling slightly. Nothing else happens. Nobody takes the arm. Nobody scans it.
Both hands stay in frame from 0:03 onward. Neither hand leaves and comes back. He does not lower the arm, does not speak, does not clench a fist, does not pull away.
````

Not gentle, not rough: he is aiming a surface. Then <<<prop_scanner>>> comes straight down, lens-first and perpendicular, and stops 4 to 5 centimeters above <<<birthcode>>>. It hovers, square and still, not touching.
````text
0:16 — One soft mechanical click from <<<prop_scanner>>>. Nothing visible happens. No beam, no flash, no light on the skin, no glow, no confirmation tone, no second click.
0:16 to 0:19 — Three full seconds held. <<<prop_scanner>>> stays exactly where it is, still perpendicular. The gloved hand stays on the wrist. Nothing moves except breathing and the tremor still running through the old man's fingers. This is the longest held moment in the shot and it reads as too long.
0:19 to 0:20 — The gloved hand simply opens. It does not lower the arm, it releases it. The forearm falls under its own weight out of the bottom of frame, loose sleeve fabric shifting with it. <<<prop_scanner>>> and both gloved hands withdraw upward out of frame. The belt line leaves the top edge. The last frames hold the empty space where the arm was.
The action happens once, in this order. It is never repeated, never reversed, never performed with the arms swapped. The roles never swap: the RIGHT hand never becomes the presented arm, the LEFT hand never pulls a sleeve.
````

## scene-08-11 · Detail shot of an older man's hands. His right hand comes down from behind his head, grips

````text
<<<birthcode>>> is tattooed into the skin. It is not a wristband, not a bracelet, not a sticker, not a printed tag, not a QR code, not a glowing implant, not a screen.
It is not backlit, not luminous, not blue-glowing, not emissive. It carries no light of its own. It is ink under skin, lit only by the room.
It reads as old and faded: soft blue-grey, bars slightly spread and blurred, edges bled into the skin.
It deforms with the skin: it curves with the wrist, distorts where the wrist creases, moves with the tremor, and shifts under the pressure of the glove. It does not react to the scanner in any way: no brightening, no pulse, no animation.
<<<prop_scanner>>> is a passive unlit tool. It emits no beam, no laser line, no red grid, no scanning sweep, no projected light, and casts nothing onto the skin. Its readout stays small, dim and monochrome, faces the officer, is never legible to camera, and is never bright enough to light his glove or the wrist. No holographic display, no floating interface, no sci-fi UI, no visible data, no green tick, no red cross.
````

## scene-08-18 · Close-up on a woman's face. She kneels with her arm already offered below frame.

`WHAT THIS FACE IS DOING` · Close-up · Handheld · 10s

Used in: [scene-08-18](../shots/scene-08/18_close-up-on-a-woman-s-face-she-kneels-with-her-arm.md)

````text
She is not frightened for herself, and it must not read as fear for herself. Her code is old and it works and she knows it. What she is carrying through these ten seconds is that her own result does not matter, because when it clears the procedure simply moves down the row, and her son is still in that row.
So the waiting is doubled: she is holding still for a machine to finish with her, and underneath that she is already somewhere else, listening past her own arm toward the next one. That is what her eyes are doing when they travel toward the row and stop short.
There is no relief when the tone sounds and no anticipation before it — those would be about her, and this is not about her. There is containment, and beneath it something that has already started and that she is not letting onto her face.
No pleading, no searching, no defiance, no glare, no theatrical dread. She does not react to the operator's arrival, the grip or the tone.
````

## scene-08-19 · The Captain finishes scanning the father's wrist, releases it, and moves down the row.

````text
The first two waits, on <<<char_dad>>> and <<<char_mum>>>, are identical in length and treatment: roughly three seconds, camera up on the face, silence, then the release. They must feel procedural and interchangeable. The audience learns the rhythm.
The third wait breaks it by duration alone. Nothing else changes: same framing logic, same camera behaviour, same silence, same absence of music. The scanner does not blink, the red band does not pulse, the light does not change, nobody moves. The horror is that the pattern simply stops completing.
Do not add any signal that the third result is different. No tone, no colour shift, no flicker, no reaction from the Captain, no sound cue, no camera push.
````

## scene-08-20 · Close-up on a woman's face, seen from behind and to her left.

````text
She is not frightened for herself, and it must not read as fear for herself. Her code is old and it works and she knows it. What she is carrying through these ten seconds is that her own result does not matter, because when it clears the procedure simply moves down the row, and her son is still in that row.
````

## scene-08-21 · Close-up on son's face, seen from behind and to his left.

`WHAT THIS FACE IS DOING` · Close-up · Handheld · 10s

Used in: [scene-08-21](../shots/scene-08/21_close-up-on-son-s-face-seen-from-behind-and-to-his.md)

````text
He is the last one in the row and there is nobody after him. Both his parents have already been through it, in front of him, and he has watched. So unlike them he has nothing to be already looking past: the only thing in these ten seconds is his own result.
His code is new and clean and he has never had a reason to doubt it. That is exactly why he is the most frightened of the three: he has no worn-in certainty to hold onto, only the assumption that it will be fine, and an assumption is thin. He is twenty and this is the first time the machine has been pointed at him with his family watching.
The performance is fear held down badly. Where his father had endurance and his mother had containment, he has neither: his body keeps failing the position and he keeps taking it back. The single upward flick of the eyes is the failure that gets furthest.
No pleading, no defiance, no theatrical dread, no weeping. The fear reads in breath, tremor, swallow and jaw. He never turns to give the camera his expression.
````

## scene-08-22 · Medium shot on the armored Captain. Two agents carry the son out of the room behind him.

`WHAT THIS SHOT IS DOING` · Medium · Handheld · 8s

Used in: [scene-08-22](../shots/scene-08/22_medium-shot-on-the-armored-captain-two-agents-carr.md)

````text
There is no face and no eyeline. Nothing about his state can be read from an expression, and none should be invented for him. The only information available is his body, and the only thing his body does is turn its head a few degrees and then not turn it back.
That is the whole performance and it must be legible: he watches, and he stays. The delay at the end — the fact that his helmet is still oriented at the empty doorway after there is nothing left to look at — is the only thing in the shot that is not procedure.
It must not read as remorse, hesitation, or a decision. Nothing softens. He is not shown to be conflicted. He is shown to be pointed at something after it has gone, for slightly longer than the job requires.
Nothing dramatises this: no push-in, no music, no sound cue, no lighting change, no reaction from anyone. Only duration and stillness.
````

## scene-09-02 · Same corridor, same drag, reverse angle.

`CHARACTER BEHAVIOR` · Close-up · Handheld · 6s

````text
<<<char_son>>>: visibly frightened and agitated throughout. Head moves — shifting left and right, looking past the camera, looking at the operators on either side, looking at nothing. Eyes wide and searching, not focused on camera lens. Breathing fast and shallow — mouth slightly open, chest moving. Occasional involuntary shoulder movement — pulling inward, twisting slightly against the grip. His body is carried forward by them — no forward volition of his own. Occasional small head jerk as the pace pulls him.

<<<team>>>: heads face forward — helmets and goggle arrays directed past camera, not at <<<char_son>>>, not at camera. Their advance is decisive and unbroken. Their grip on him is firm and unchanging. No head turn, no reaction, no acknowledgment of camera.
````

## scene-09-03 · Same corridor, reverse angle. Camera in front of the three figures, walking backward as th

`CHARACTER PERFORMANCE — CRITICAL` · Close-up · Handheld · 6s

Used in: [scene-09-03](../shots/scene-09/03_same-corridor-reverse-angle-camera-in-front-of-the.md)

````text
This is the core of the shot. <<<char_son>>> does not perform fear. He is past it.

Baseline state, 0:00–0:06: face nearly still. Eyes open, unfocused, aimed at a point past the camera that does not exist. No searching, no darting, no looking around. He is not taking in his surroundings — he has stopped taking anything in. Brow relaxed, not furrowed. Mouth closed or barely parted. Breathing slow and shallow — not fast, not panicked. His body is completely passive between the two operators, carried, offering nothing.

Two involuntary breaks, and only two:

Break one, approximately 0:02 — a swallow. Throat moves once. The only sign that his body is still processing anything. He does not react to it.

Break two, approximately 0:04–0:05 — his jaw gives way for less than a second. The muscle loses hold, mouth opens slightly, then closes. No sound. No expression change around it. Then back to the baseline blankness.

Optionally a single slow blink somewhere in the middle — longer than a normal blink, eyes closed for almost a full beat, then open again to the same unfocused nothing.

That is the entire performance. No head shaking. No twisting. No struggling. No wide eyes. No open-mouthed terror. The fear is legible precisely because it has nowhere to go.

<<<team>>>: heads face forward past camera. Advance decisive and unbroken. Grip firm and unchanging. No reaction, no acknowledgment of camera.
````

## scene-09-04 · Same corridor, reverse angle, tighter. Camera in front of the three figures, walking backw

`CHARACTER PERFORMANCE — CRITICAL` · Close-up · Handheld · 6s

Used in: [scene-09-04](../shots/scene-09/04_same-corridor-reverse-angle-tighter-camera-in-fron.md)

````text
0:00–0:02 — Head up. Chin level. Eyes open, unfocused, aimed past the camera at nothing that exists. Face nearly still. No searching, no darting. Brow relaxed. Breathing slow and shallow. He is present in posture only.

0:02–0:04 — The head begins to lower. Slow, not a collapse — the neck simply stops holding. Chin drops toward chest. Hair falls further across the forehead. The eyes go with it — now aimed downward, still unfocused. No expression change during the descent. It is not a decision. It is a giving out.

0:04–0:06 — Head fully lowered. Then, from that lowered position, a small refusal: he shakes his head no. Slow, small amplitude — two or three degrees each way, one or two cycles. The movement does not lift his head. It is a no made from below, to nobody, about nothing that can be changed. His eyes stay down throughout. No expression on the face during the shake. Then it stops. Head stays down. Final frame: lowered head, hair across face, eyes down.

No wide eyes. No grimace. No open-mouthed terror. No struggling. No looking at the camera at any point.

<<<team>>>: heads face forward past camera, above the frame line. Advance decisive and unbroken. Grip firm and unchanging. No reaction, no acknowledgment.
````

## scene-09-05 · A decayed institutional corridor. An old man walks after two operators who are carrying so

`CHARACTER PERFORMANCE` · Close-up · Handheld · 4s

Used in: [scene-09-05](../shots/scene-09/05_a-decayed-institutional-corridor-an-old-man-walks.md)

````text
<<<char_dad>>> is visibly agitated. The face is working and will not hold still. Brow drawn tight and shifting. Jaw clenching and releasing. Mouth opening slightly then closing, opening again. Eyes wide, wet at the rims but not spilling, locked forward on his own line of travel past the camera. He does not look at the camera. He does not look away.

Breathing broken — sharp intakes, uneven exhales, throat visibly moving. Micro-tremor in lower lip and chin. Head jerks slightly once or twice — not from the walk, from the body failing to contain what is in it.

He keeps walking, steady, without stopping. The agitation is entirely in face and breath; the legs hold their pace regardless. That mismatch is the shot.
````

## scene-09-06 · A decayed institutional corridor. An old man walks after two operators who are carrying so

`CHARACTER PERFORMANCE` · Close-up · Handheld · 10s

Used in: [scene-09-06](../shots/scene-09/06_a-decayed-institutional-corridor-an-old-man-walks.md)

````text
He is coming apart in the face and holding together in the legs. Brow drawn tight and shifting. Jaw clenching between deliveries, shaking during them. Mouth opening before each line and staying slightly open after. Eyes wide, wet at the rims, spilling by the third delivery or close to it — but he does not wipe them, does not acknowledge them. Eyes locked forward on his own line of travel past the camera at all times. He does not look at the camera. He does not look away.

Micro-tremor in the lower lip and chin, worsening across the 10 seconds. Head jerks slightly with the effort of each shout — not from the walk, from the body forcing sound out of a throat that is failing.

He keeps walking, steady, without stopping, without speeding up. That mismatch — the legs unbroken, the face and voice destroyed — is the shot.
````

## scene-10-02 · An armored officer comes slowly into an empty apartment, pushes the door shut behind him, 

`HOW HE LOOKS AROUND — NO FACE, NO EYELINE` · Wide · Handheld · 15s

Used in: [scene-10-02](../shots/scene-10/02_an-armored-officer-comes-slowly-into-an-empty-apar.md)

````text
He has no visible eyes and no readable expression. Nothing about his attention can be shown through a face, and none must be invented for him.
Searching is expressed ONLY through the orientation of the helmet and the alignment of the body behind it:
- The helmet stops. It holds on one direction for a beat, long enough to read as attention, then moves to the next. It does not sweep continuously and it does not scan smoothly like a machine.
- Each move is a real lateral rotation of the neck carrying a heavy helmet: it starts, decelerates, and settles with a fractional overshoot and correction.
- The torso follows the helmet a beat late on the larger turns, the plate carrier and the weapon coming round after the head has already arrived.
- He never returns twice to the same direction. The sequence of directions is methodical and does not double back. This is what makes it read as procedure rather than as nervousness.
- His shoulders stay level and his stance stays balanced. He does not flinch, does not startle, does not react to anything.
No head tilts to suggest curiosity, no lowering to suggest doubt, no quick jerks to suggest fear. The attention is flat and administrative.
Because the camera is locked to his profile, we never see what he is looking at. The room he is checking stays dissolved behind him for the entire take.
````

## scene-10-04 · An armored officer pushes a half-open apartment door with the muzzle of his weapon and com

`THE BEAR` · Medium · Handheld · 15s

Used in: [scene-10-04](../shots/scene-10/04_an-armored-officer-pushes-a-half-open-apartment-do.md)

````text
<<<prop_bear>>> sits on the low striped sofa against the far wall, at the end of the seat nearer the window, propped against the back cushion and slumped slightly to one side. It is the only object in the room that belongs to a person rather than to the building.
It was left, not placed: it sits at an angle, one arm caught behind it, sagging into the cushion under its own softness. Nothing about the way it sits looks arranged or symmetrical.
It does not move at any point. Nothing touches it, nothing disturbs it, no draught moves the plush. It is not knocked over, not picked up, not looked at.
Because this is a medium shot, the sofa is in frame for most of the take and the bear is legible for most of the take. It is not revealed and it does not arrive: it is simply there, in the background, from the moment the sofa enters frame until the end. The audience has time to find it on their own.
<<<captain-agentv4>>> walks past it and does not stop. His helmet does not turn toward it and does not hold on it. He does not touch it, does not point the weapon at it, does not acknowledge it in any way. If it registers with him at all, nothing in his body shows it.
The bear is legible to the audience for a long time and invisible to him for all of it. That asymmetry is the point of the shot.
Exactly one bear. No other toys, no other personal objects, no clothing, no children's things anywhere else in the room.
````

## scene-10-04 · An armored officer pushes a half-open apartment door with the muzzle of his weapon and com

`THE SOFA AND THE BEAR.` · Medium · Handheld · 15s

Used in: [scene-10-04](../shots/scene-10/04_an-armored-officer-pushes-a-half-open-apartment-do.md)

````text
The sofa sits under the window, so the near end of the seat catches the window light and the far end falls away into the room's shadow. <<<prop_bear>>> is slumped at the lit end. This is not a decision made for the bear — it is simply where the light from that window lands on that sofa, and anything left there would sit in it.
Because of that, the bear is the palest thing on the far wall: worn mid-brown plush against dark striped upholstery, a small warm-neutral mass in a room of mint green, cold grey and desaturated olive. It does not glow, it is not warmed, it has no accent, no rim, no separate source, no practical near it, no light of its own. It is simply the one soft, pale, hand-worn object in a hard cold room, and the room's own light finds it.
At medium framing it is small in the frame but clearly legible: the slumped posture, the caught arm, the matted pile, the two dark dead points of the eye and nose. A film of dust sits in the pile, the same dust that is on the sofa and the window sill. It has been there a while. It is not clean, not lit, not precious.
It casts one small soft shadow onto the cushion behind it, from the window, consistent with everything else in the room.
````

## scene-10-04 · An armored officer pushes a half-open apartment door with the muzzle of his weapon and com

`HOW HE PASSES IT.` · Medium · Handheld · 15s

Used in: [scene-10-04](../shots/scene-10/04_an-armored-officer-pushes-a-half-open-apartment-do.md)

````text
As <<<captain-agentv4>>> crosses left to right, his body occludes the lit end of the sofa for a moment and then clears it. The bear is hidden behind him, then in the light again as he moves on, exactly as it was. That is caused entirely by his mass moving through the frame, not by focus, not by exposure, not by a camera move, and not by anything changing in the room.
His own light does not change as he passes it and no light from him falls on it. Nothing about the two of them being in the same frame is marked.

The rest of the room.
The light crosses him from the side as he moves. The dome helmet catches a soft cold contour along its upper edge, travelling over the curve as the head rotates — that moving highlight is one of the clearest indicators of each turn. As he crosses left to right the corridor edge falls off his back and the window becomes his key. The olive fabric and plate carrier read dark and desaturated and absorb the light. The helmet stays dull matte grey-white, never bright, never glossy, with visible field wear. The black suppressor stays matte and takes no highlight.
The four-lens cluster reads as deep near-black glass with at most a faint passive reflection of the window or the corridor opening.
Exposure is set for the cold window daylight, not lifted. He is allowed to sit dark, and so is the bear when he is in front of it.
No flat front light, no beauty fill, no studio key, no rim, no light from camera position, no warm tone anywhere, no red, no saturated colour of any kind, no lighting change timed to a helmet turn or to the bear.
````

## scene-10-05 · Close detail of a gloved hand and a child's teddy bear.

`THE HANDS — PROFESSIONAL, THEN TOO LONG` · Extreme close-up · Handheld · 8s

Used in: [scene-10-05](../shots/scene-10/05_close-detail-of-a-gloved-hand-and-a-child-s-teddy.md)

````text
This is the first time in this sequence he has touched anything, and it must not be softened.
Both hands stay exactly as they were on the wrists earlier: no tremor, no hesitation, no regripping, no drift. Economical, unhurried, slightly bored. The reach is direct. The grip is a working grip, the same fist he closes on anything.
Do NOT make the hands tender. No stroking, no smoothing the fur, no thumb passing over the plush, no cradling, no two-handed handling, no adjusting it to sit better, no straightening the caught arm, no turning it to see its face properly. He handles it the way he handled the arms: like a thing, with one hand, because the other one is busy.
The ONLY departure from procedure is duration. He should have looked at it for a moment and moved on. Instead the fist stops at 0:05 and stays stopped for three full seconds, holding it up, doing nothing. Those three seconds are the entire event of this shot and they are longer than everything that came before them. It is expressed as time, not as gesture.
Nothing else changes. No sigh, no shift, no slump, no lowering of the hand, no gesture of recognition, no loosening grip on the weapon. The hands do not tighten and do not loosen. They simply do not move on.
````

## scene-10-05 · Close detail of a gloved hand and a child's teddy bear.

`THE BEAR — WHAT IT DOES IN ONE FIST` · Extreme close-up · Handheld · 8s

Used in: [scene-10-05](../shots/scene-10/05_close-detail-of-a-gloved-hand-and-a-child-s-teddy.md)

````text
<<<prop_bear>>> is soft, unstuffed enough to be floppy, and old. It behaves accordingly and this behaviour is the whole physical content of the shot.
Held by the middle in one fist, it has nothing supporting it: the head lolls forward on the soft neck seam, both arms hang down, the legs hang down, and the whole body sags around the point where it is gripped. It looks badly held, because it is.
The body compresses in the fist and stays compressed while the pressure is on, the plush bulging out between the fingers. The limbs swing on the lift and damp to rest a beat after the hand stops.
The plush reads at this magnification: matted flat at the belly and the muzzle where it has been held for years, still curly and fuller at the back of the head and the outer arms, individual fibres catching the cold daylight along the top surfaces. A film of household dust sits in the pile. Seams visible. One dark stitched eye, dead and flat, and a dark oval felt nose, worn shiny in one spot from handling.
It reflects nothing and lights nothing. It is not clean, not new, not precious, not lit for effect.
When he releases pressure it does not fully recover: the body stays slightly out of shape where the fist pressed. That deformation is still there at the end of the take.
The bear does not move on its own at any point. No animation, no life, no cute behaviour, no head tilt of its own.
````

Used in: [scene-10-05](../shots/scene-10/05_close-detail-of-a-gloved-hand-and-a-child-s-teddy.md)

````text
Olive tactical glove leather and reinforced fabric: hard, creased, dulled with wear, scuffed at the knuckles, dust and grime in the seams. It creases and stretches over the knuckles as the fist closes.
Black weapon body: matte, machined, hard-edged, cold. Held by the other hand at the edge of frame, unglamorous and unmoving.
Worn mid-brown plush: soft, matted, dusty, giving completely under pressure.
Where the glove meets the plush, the plush is the thing that deforms. The glove does not. The weapon does not. This asymmetry is the point of the close-up and it must be visible in every frame from 0:02 onward.
No warmth, no softening of the light between them, no glow, no lens effect that makes it beautiful.
````

`THE WEAPON GOES ON THE SLING` · Extreme close-up · Handheld · 8s

Used in: [scene-10-06](../shots/scene-10/06_close-detail-of-gloved-hands-and-a-child-s-teddy-b.md)

````text
To free both hands he lets the carbine go. He does not put it down and does not stow it: he simply releases his grip and it drops onto its sling, hanging across his chest with the muzzle low, swinging once and settling.
This happens at 0:02, before he touches the bear, and it is a small clear physical event: the weight transferring from his hands to the strap, a short drop, the sling snapping taut.
Once released, the carbine stays hanging for the rest of the take. He never takes hold of it again, never steadies it, never pushes it aside. It hangs at the lower edge of frame or just out of it, dark and soft, moving only when his body moves.
Both hands are then free and stay free until the end of the take.
He does not lay the weapon on the sofa, does not lean it against anything, does not sling it over a shoulder, does not let it fall to the floor.
````

## scene-10-07 · Close-up on an armored officer's face, seated.

`THE REVEAL — IN TWO STAGES` · Close-up · Handheld · 15s

Used in: [scene-10-07](../shots/scene-10/07_close-up-on-an-armored-officer-s-face-seated.md)

````text
This is the first time this face has been seen. It arrives in two parts and neither is dramatised.
STAGE ONE — the helmet comes off. The upper face is bare: forehead, eyes, brow, temples, damp disordered hair. The nose, mouth and jaw are still behind the olive gaiter, per <<<image_1>>>. Most of a face, and the part that is showing does not look at anything.
STAGE TWO — the gaiter comes down. The rest arrives: nose, mouth, chin, the short beard with grey in it, the flush where the fabric pressed.
Between the two stages there are three seconds with the eyes bare and the mouth still covered. That gap is the structure of the shot: the audience gets his eyes and has to wait for his mouth.
Neither stage is treated as a reveal. No slow lift held for effect, no beat of stillness as the face appears, no light change, no music, no camera move, no exhale timed to either uncovering. He simply takes off two pieces of kit and both times a bit more of him is there.
````

## scene-10-08 · Close-up on a child's teddy bear. A man seated on a sofa sets down the helmet in his lap, 

`WHY THE HOLD IS THE SHOT` · Close-up · Handheld · 10s

Used in: [scene-10-08](../shots/scene-10/08_close-up-on-a-child-s-teddy-bear-a-man-seated-on-a.md)

````text
He put down a helmet, crossed his own body to reach an object, picked it up, and then did nothing with it.
Do not fill the hold. No gesture arrives. No expression arrives — and none could, because the face is soft. No camera move marks it. No light changes. No sound comes in. Nothing swells.
The only thing that happens is that the shot does not cut away from a teddy bear for far longer than a shot of a teddy bear should last. That is the entire effect and it is made of duration alone.
````
